Logit biases allow you to directly add or subtract values from specific token logits before sampling, increasing or decreasing the probability of particular tokens. OpenAI's API exposes this as a dictionary mapping token IDs to bias values (e.g., {token_id: -100} effectively bans that token). Use cases include: forcing or preventing specific words or formatting tokens (ban markdown, force a specific language), hard-constraining output to a set of valid options (e.g., for classification, bias heavily toward the valid label tokens), preventing generation of specific named entities or sensitive information, and steering style without prompt engineering. Setting a bias to -100 effectively bans a token; +5 to +10 strongly encourages it. It is a surgical tool — less flexible than prompt engineering but more reliable for hard constraints on specific tokens. The limitation is that it operates on tokens, not words (a word may be multiple tokens), requiring careful tokenizer-aware implementation.

**Q84. Explain speculative decoding — how does it speed up generation without changing output distribution?**
Speculative decoding (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023) uses a small, fast "draft" model to generate a sequence of k candidate tokens, then verifies all k tokens in a single forward pass of the large "target" model. The target model computes the probability of each drafted token conditioned on the preceding context; tokens that the target model agrees with (probability above a stochastic threshold based on the ratio of target/draft probabilities) are accepted, and generation continues from the first rejected token. Crucially, the acceptance criterion is designed so that the final output distribution is mathematically identical to what the target model would have produced alone — there is zero quality degradation. The speedup comes from the fact that verifying k tokens in parallel (one forward pass) is much faster than generating k tokens sequentially (k forward passes) because transformer inference is memory-bandwidth-bound, not compute-bound. Typical speedups are 2–3x. This technique is widely deployed in production (used by Google's systems, vLLM, and others).

**Q85. How do you tune sampling for creative tasks vs factual tasks?**
For factual tasks (QA, extraction, code, math), use low temperature (0–0.3), top-p of 0.9 or lower, no repetition penalty (or very mild), and consider greedy decoding (T=0) when determinism matters — the goal is to stay close to the model's most confident outputs and minimize hallucination risk. For creative tasks (story writing, brainstorming, poetry), use higher temperature (0.7–1.0), top-p of 0.9–0.95, mild repetition penalty (1.1–1.2) to prevent loops, and consider min-p (0.05–0.1) or typical sampling for more natural text. For conversational use, a middle ground of T=0.5–0.7 with top-p=0.9 balances coherence and naturalness. A useful mental model: temperature controls how creative/risky the output is, top-p/min-p controls how many options are on the table, and repetition penalty prevents degeneration. Always test parameter combinations on your specific task — optimal values are empirical and task-dependent.

**Q86. What is structured decoding / constrained generation (Outlines, Guidance)?**
Structured decoding constrains the LLM's generation to always produce valid output conforming to a formal grammar — a JSON schema, regex pattern, or context-free grammar — by masking invalid tokens at each generation step. Libraries like Outlines (dottxt) and Guidance (Microsoft) precompile the grammar/schema into a finite-state machine or pushdown automaton, and at each token generation step, they compute which tokens are valid continuations given the current parse state, setting all invalid token logits to negative infinity before sampling. This guarantees 100% syntactically valid output without retries or post-processing, solving the reliability problem of hoping the model produces valid JSON. The overhead is minimal since the grammar-based token masking is fast compared to the model forward pass. Outlines integrates with HuggingFace, vLLM, and other serving frameworks. OpenAI's structured outputs and Anthropic's tool use provide similar guarantees at the API level by constraining generation server-side to match a provided JSON schema.

---

### Part 2: Fine-Tuning

**Q87. Difference between pre-training, fine-tuning, and instruction tuning?**
Pre-training trains a model from scratch (or near-scratch) on a massive corpus of text using self-supervised objectives like next-token prediction, teaching the model general language understanding, world knowledge, and reasoning capabilities — this is the most expensive phase, costing millions of dollars and requiring trillions of tokens. Fine-tuning takes a pre-trained model and continues training on a smaller, task-specific dataset to specialize its behavior for a particular domain or task, adapting the general model to a narrow use case. Instruction tuning is a specific type of fine-tuning where the training data consists of (instruction, response) pairs that teach the model to follow human instructions rather than just predict the next token — this is what transforms a base model (GPT-3) into an assistant (ChatGPT). These stages are typically sequential: pre-train on web data, then instruction-tune on curated (instruction, response) pairs, then optionally align with RLHF/DPO on human preference data.

**Q88. When is full fine-tuning justified vs parameter-efficient methods?**
Full fine-tuning (updating all model parameters) is justified when you have abundant high-quality training data (100K+ examples), sufficient compute (multiple GPUs), the domain is significantly different from pre-training data (specialized scientific or legal language), you need maximum performance and can accept the infrastructure cost, or you're building a product where the fine-tuned model is a core competitive advantage. Parameter-efficient methods (LoRA, QLoRA, adapters) are preferred when compute or memory is limited (single GPU fine-tuning), the task is close to the model's existing capabilities, you need to maintain multiple task-specific variants (LoRA adapters are small and swappable), data is limited (PEFT methods often regularize better with small datasets, reducing overfitting), or you want rapid experimentation. In practice, QLoRA gives 90–99% of full fine-tuning quality at a fraction of the cost for most use cases, making it the default starting point.

**Q89. Explain LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) mathematically.**
LoRA (Hu et al., 2021) freezes all original model weights W₀ ∈ R^(d×d) and adds a trainable low-rank decomposition: W = W₀ + ΔW = W₀ + BA, where B ∈ R^(d×r) and A ∈ R^(r×d), with rank r << d. The forward pass becomes h = (W₀ + BA)x = W₀x + BAx. Only A and B are trained, reducing trainable parameters from d² to 2·d·r — for d=4096 and r=16, this is a reduction from ~16.7M to ~131K parameters per adapted weight matrix (a 128x reduction). A is initialized with a random Gaussian and B is initialized to zero, so ΔW = BA = 0 at the start of training, meaning the model begins with its original behavior and gradually adapts. A scaling factor α/r is applied to ΔW (α is a hyperparameter, typically set equal to r or 2r) to control the magnitude of the adaptation. At inference, BA can be merged into W₀ with no additional latency cost.

**Q90. What is the rank parameter (r) in LoRA? How do you choose it?**
The rank r determines the dimensionality of the low-rank adaptation matrices A and B, directly controlling the expressiveness and parameter count of the adaptation. Higher r allows more complex adaptations but increases memory, compute, and overfitting risk; lower r is more parameter-efficient but may underfit for complex tasks. Common guidelines: r=8–16 is a good starting point for most tasks; r=4 works for simple style or formatting adaptations; r=32–64 may be needed for significant domain shifts (medical, legal) or learning complex new behaviors; r=128+ is rarely needed and approaches full fine-tuning territory. The choice depends on the gap between the base model's behavior and the target behavior: the larger the gap, the higher the rank needed. A practical approach is to start with r=16, evaluate, and increase only if performance plateaus. The original LoRA paper showed that ranks as low as 4 achieved surprisingly strong results on many tasks, suggesting that task-specific adaptations are often low-dimensional.

**Q91. Which layers do you typically apply LoRA to and why?**
Most commonly, LoRA is applied to the attention projection matrices (Q, K, V, and output projection) because these directly control what the model attends to and are empirically the most impactful layers for task adaptation. The original LoRA paper found that adapting Q and V projections gave the best results, though subsequent practice has shown that adapting all four attention projections (Q, K, V, O) plus the FFN layers (gate, up, down projections) often yields better results — this "all-linear-layers" approach is the default in most modern LoRA training setups. Applying LoRA to more layers increases trainable parameters but also increases expressiveness. The embedding and language model head are typically not adapted (they're large and less impactful), though adapting them can help when the task involves new vocabulary. Tools like Axolotl and Unsloth make it easy to configure which layers receive LoRA adapters via a `target_modules` parameter.

**Q92. What is QLoRA? How does it combine quantization with LoRA?**
QLoRA (Dettmers et al., 2023) enables fine-tuning of large models on a single GPU by combining three techniques: (1) quantizing the base model weights to 4-bit NormalFloat (NF4) precision, reducing the frozen model's memory footprint by ~4x; (2) applying LoRA adapters in the original higher precision (float16 or bfloat16) so the trainable parameters maintain full gradient fidelity; and (3) using "double quantization" (quantizing the quantization constants) and paged optimizers (offloading optimizer states to CPU when GPU memory is full) for additional memory savings. During the forward pass, the 4-bit weights are dequantized to 16-bit for computation, the LoRA adapters are added, and gradients flow only through the LoRA parameters. This enabled fine-tuning a 65B parameter model on a single 48GB GPU, which was impossible before. The quality loss from 4-bit quantization is minimal (NF4 is information-theoretically optimal for normally-distributed weights), making QLoRA the default method for resource-constrained fine-tuning.

**Q93. Memory savings of QLoRA vs full fine-tuning vs standard LoRA?**
For a 7B parameter model: full fine-tuning requires ~28GB just for the model in fp16 plus ~56GB for optimizer states (Adam stores two states per parameter) and ~28GB for gradients, totaling ~112GB+ across GPUs. Standard LoRA in fp16 requires ~28GB for the frozen model plus a small amount for LoRA parameters and their optimizer states (a few hundred MB for typical rank), totaling ~30GB. QLoRA requires ~3.5GB for the 4-bit quantized model plus LoRA parameters in fp16, totaling ~6–8GB, fitting comfortably on a single consumer GPU (e.g., RTX 3090 24GB can fine-tune 7B; RTX 4090 can handle 13B). The ratios roughly are: full fine-tuning needs ~16 bytes/param, LoRA needs ~2 bytes/param (frozen weights only), QLoRA needs ~0.5 bytes/param for frozen weights. This means QLoRA provides approximately 15–20x memory reduction over full fine-tuning, which is why it democratized fine-tuning to consumer hardware.

**Q94. Name PEFT methods beyond LoRA. Compare them.**
Major PEFT methods include: LoRA/QLoRA (low-rank weight updates, most popular, ~0.1–1% params); Adapters (Houlsby et al., 2019 — small bottleneck layers inserted between transformer sublayers, ~2–4% params, slightly increase inference latency); Prefix Tuning (Li & Liang, 2021 — learns continuous "virtual token" embeddings prepended to the key/value pairs in attention, ~0.1% params); Prompt Tuning (Lester et al., 2021 — learns soft prompt embeddings prepended to the input, ~0.01% params, simplest but weakest); IA3 (Liu et al., 2022 — learns element-wise rescaling vectors for key, value, and FFN activations, even fewer params than LoRA); and (IA)³/DoRA (weight-decomposed LoRA). LoRA dominates in practice due to its strong quality-efficiency tradeoff, no inference latency overhead (weights merge), and broad tooling support. Adapters are competitive but add inference latency since they cannot be merged. Prefix/prompt tuning are the most parameter-efficient but generally underperform LoRA.

**Q95. Explain prefix tuning and prompt tuning as PEFT methods.**
Prefix tuning (Li & Liang, 2021) prepends learned continuous vectors to the key and value representations at every transformer layer, effectively giving the model additional "virtual attention context" that influences how all subsequent tokens are processed. It learns a separate small network (MLP) that generates these prefix vectors, adding about 0.1% trainable parameters. Prompt tuning (Lester et al., 2021) is simpler: it only prepends learned soft token embeddings to the input embedding layer (not every layer), adding just ~0.01% parameters. Prompt tuning performs poorly at small model scales but approaches fine-tuning quality at very large scales (10B+ parameters), suggesting that large models are better at leveraging the limited information in soft prompts. Both methods keep all model weights frozen and are extremely parameter-efficient, but they generally underperform LoRA for the same compute budget and add inference complexity since the virtual tokens consume context window space. They are mainly of historical and research interest now.

**Q96. What is adapter tuning? How do adapters differ from LoRA?**
Adapter tuning (Houlsby et al., 2019) inserts small trainable bottleneck modules between existing transformer sublayers. Each adapter consists of a down-projection (d → r), a nonlinear activation, and an up-projection (r → d) with a residual connection, typically placed after both the attention and FFN sublayers. Unlike LoRA, which modifies existing weight matrices via an additive low-rank decomposition (and can be merged at inference for zero overhead), adapters add new sequential computation that cannot be merged into the original weights, adding latency at inference proportional to the number of adapter layers. Adapters are more expressive than LoRA of equivalent parameter count because they include a nonlinearity, but the inference overhead makes them less practical for serving. LoRA's ability to merge adapter weights into the base model with zero inference cost is its decisive advantage, which is why LoRA has largely displaced adapters in practice despite adapters being introduced earlier.

**Q97. How does DoRA improve upon LoRA?**
DoRA (Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation, Liu et al., 2024) decomposes each weight matrix into its magnitude and direction components — W = m · (V / ||V||) — and applies LoRA only to the directional component V while making the magnitude m a separately trainable scalar. The insight comes from analyzing how full fine-tuning updates weights versus how LoRA updates weights: full fine-tuning shows a pattern of large directional changes with proportional magnitude changes, while standard LoRA tends to couple magnitude and direction updates in a suboptimal way. By decomposing these, DoRA can better approximate full fine-tuning's learning dynamics with the same number of parameters as LoRA. Empirically, DoRA consistently outperforms LoRA by 1–3% across tasks at the same rank, with negligible additional overhead. It is supported in the PEFT library (Hugging Face) and Unsloth, and is a drop-in replacement for LoRA that is worth trying when squeezing out additional quality matters.

**Q98. Explain RLHF end-to-end. The full pipeline.**
RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) is a multi-stage pipeline: (1) SFT (Supervised Fine-Tuning): a pre-trained base model is fine-tuned on high-quality (instruction, response) pairs to create an initial assistant model. (2) Reward Model Training: human annotators rank multiple model responses to the same prompt by quality (e.g., response A > B > C), and a separate model is trained to predict a scalar reward score that matches these rankings using a Bradley-Terry pairwise loss. (3) RL Optimization: the SFT model is further optimized using PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization) to generate responses that maximize the reward model's score, with a KL divergence penalty against the SFT model (the "reference model") to prevent the policy from deviating too far and exploiting reward model weaknesses. The result is a model that generates responses aligned with human preferences. This pipeline was introduced by Ouyang et al. (2022) for InstructGPT and remains foundational, though DPO has simplified the process for many use cases.

**Q99. Role of the reward model in RLHF? How is it trained?**
The reward model serves as a learned proxy for human judgment, providing a scalar score indicating how good a response is for a given prompt. It is typically initialized from the SFT model (or a similar pre-trained model) with the language model head replaced by a scalar regression head. Training data consists of pairwise comparisons: for each prompt, human annotators rank 2+ model responses by quality, and the model is trained with a Bradley-Terry pairwise ranking loss: L = -log(σ(r(x, y_w) - r(x, y_l))), where y_w is the preferred response and y_l is the rejected response. This loss pushes the model to assign higher scores to human-preferred responses. Key challenges include reward hacking (the RL policy exploits weaknesses in the reward model to get high scores without actually being better), distribution shift (the reward model was trained on SFT-quality outputs but must evaluate RL-optimized outputs), and annotation quality/consistency. Larger reward models generally provide more robust signal, and Anthropic has explored constitutional AI as an alternative to human annotation.

**Q100. Explain PPO as used in RLHF. Why PPO?**
PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization, Schulman et al., 2017) is the RL algorithm used to optimize the language model policy to maximize reward model scores. In RLHF: the model (policy) generates a response, the reward model scores it, and PPO updates the policy to increase the probability of high-reward responses while clipping the update to prevent destabilizingly large changes — the clipped objective is: L = min(r_t(θ) · Â_t, clip(r_t(θ), 1-ε, 1+ε) · Â_t), where r_t is the probability ratio between new and old policies and Â_t is the advantage estimate. A KL penalty term KL(π_θ || π_ref) is added to prevent the policy from drifting too far from the reference (SFT) model. PPO was chosen for RLHF because it is stable (clipping prevents catastrophic updates), sample-efficient relative to other policy gradient methods, well-studied with reliable implementations, and handles the high-dimensional discrete action space of text generation. However, PPO in RLHF is complex to implement correctly (requiring 4 models in memory: policy, reference, reward, value), which motivated simpler alternatives like DPO.

**Q101. What is DPO? How does it eliminate the reward model?**
DPO (Direct Preference Optimization, Rafailov et al., 2023) reformulates the RLHF objective to eliminate the explicit reward model and RL training loop entirely. Through a mathematical derivation, DPO shows that the optimal policy under the RLHF objective (maximize reward minus KL penalty) can be expressed as a closed-form function of the policy's own log-probabilities. This yields a simple supervised loss: L_DPO = -log σ(β · [log(π_θ(y_w|x)/π_ref(y_w|x)) - log(π_θ(y_l|x)/π_ref(y_l|x))]), where y_w and y_l are preferred and rejected responses, π_θ is the trained policy, π_ref is the frozen reference model, and β controls the strength of the KL constraint. This is a standard cross-entropy-style loss that can be optimized with normal gradient descent — no RL, no reward model, no value function. You just need pairs of (prompt, preferred response, rejected response) and two forward passes through the policy and reference model. This dramatically simplifies the alignment pipeline from four models to two.

**Q102. RLHF vs DPO — tradeoffs, performance, simplicity?**
DPO is dramatically simpler to implement (supervised learning with paired data vs. a four-model RL pipeline), more computationally efficient (2 models vs. 4 in memory, no reward model training or sampling loop), and more stable to train (no reward hacking, no PPO hyperparameter sensitivity). RLHF with PPO can potentially achieve better results because the reward model provides a richer training signal — the RL policy can generate novel responses and get reward feedback, enabling exploration beyond the fixed preference dataset. In practice, DPO achieves competitive or superior results on most benchmarks and is the default choice for most teams due to its simplicity. However, RLHF retains advantages for very large-scale training (frontier models) where exploration and online data generation matter, and when the reward model can be iteratively improved. Most open-source alignment (Zephyr, Tulu, OpenChat) uses DPO, while frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google) still use RLHF variants for their flagship models, sometimes combining both approaches.

**Q103. What is ORPO (Odds Ratio Preference Optimization)?**
ORPO (Hong et al., 2024) combines supervised fine-tuning and preference alignment into a single training stage by adding a preference-aware penalty directly to the SFT loss. The loss is: L_ORPO = L_SFT + λ · L_OR, where L_SFT is the standard cross-entropy on the preferred response, and L_OR is a log odds ratio term that penalizes the model for assigning higher likelihood to rejected responses relative to preferred ones. The odds ratio compares the odds of generating the preferred vs rejected response, providing a reference-free preference signal. Unlike DPO, ORPO does not need a separate reference model (eliminating one model from memory), and unlike RLHF, it needs no reward model or RL loop. By combining SFT and alignment in one pass, ORPO reduces the total training pipeline from two or three stages to one. Results show competitive performance with DPO while being simpler and more memory-efficient, making it attractive for resource-constrained settings.

**Q104. What is KTO (Kahneman-Tversky Optimization)?**
KTO (Ethayarajh et al., 2024) is a preference optimization method that only requires binary signals (good/bad) rather than pairwise preferences (A better than B), inspired by Kahneman and Tversky's prospect theory from behavioral economics. While DPO requires paired data (the same prompt with both a chosen and rejected response), KTO works with unpaired data — a response simply labeled as "desirable" or "undesirable," which is much easier and cheaper to collect. KTO's loss function applies asymmetric weighting based on prospect theory: losses (bad outputs) are weighted more heavily than gains (good outputs), reflecting the human bias of loss aversion. This is practically significant because most real-world feedback data is binary (thumbs up/down, flags) rather than pairwise comparisons, and creating paired preference datasets is expensive. KTO achieves competitive results with DPO while being applicable to the much more abundant unpaired feedback data.

**Q105. Catastrophic forgetting in fine-tuning — how do you mitigate it?**
Catastrophic forgetting occurs when fine-tuning on a specific task degrades the model's performance on other tasks and its general capabilities. Mitigation strategies include: using parameter-efficient methods like LoRA (which freeze most parameters, inherently preserving general knowledge); adding a KL divergence penalty against the original model during training (as done in RLHF); mixing general-purpose data into the fine-tuning dataset (e.g., 10–20% of the original pre-training data or instruction data); using low learning rates (1e-5 to 5e-5 for full fine-tuning, 1e-4 to 3e-4 for LoRA); early stopping based on validation loss on both the target task and a general benchmark; elastic weight consolidation (EWC), which penalizes changes to parameters important for previous tasks; and model merging techniques that combine the fine-tuned model with the base model. In practice, QLoRA with a conservative learning rate and a mixed training dataset is the most reliable and practical approach.

**Q106. Data formats for instruction tuning? (Alpaca, ShareGPT, etc.)**
The major data formats are: Alpaca format — a JSON list of {"instruction": ..., "input": ..., "output": ...} dicts, simple and widely supported, best for single-turn tasks. ShareGPT/conversation format — {"conversations": [{"from": "human", "value": ...}, {"from": "gpt", "value": ...}, ...]} for multi-turn dialogues, which is the natural format for chat model training. OpenAI format — {"messages": [{"role": "system", "content": ...}, {"role": "user", "content": ...}, {"role": "assistant", "content": ...}]}, widely used in API-compatible training. ChatML — a tokenized format with special tokens like <|im_start|>user\n...<|im_end|> used by many open-source models. For preference tuning, the format extends to include "chosen" and "rejected" response pairs. Training frameworks like Axolotl, Unsloth, and TRL support multiple formats and handle the tokenization/masking automatically. The key consideration is proper loss masking — training loss should only be computed on the assistant responses, not on the prompts or system messages.

**Q107. How much data for effective fine-tuning? How to assess data quality?**
The amount depends heavily on the task: for style/format adaptation, as few as 100–500 high-quality examples can be sufficient with LoRA; for teaching new knowledge or complex behaviors, 1,000–10,000 examples is typical; for significant domain adaptation, 10,000–100,000+ examples may be needed. Quality consistently matters more than quantity — the "LIMA" paper (Zhou et al., 2023) showed that just 1,000 carefully curated examples produced a surprisingly strong instruction-following model. Assess data quality by: manually reviewing a random sample (100+ examples), checking for consistency in formatting and style, removing duplicates and near-duplicates (using embedding similarity), filtering out low-quality or incorrect responses (use a strong LLM as a judge), ensuring diversity of topics and difficulty levels, and validating that the data distribution matches your target use case. A practical workflow is to start with a small high-quality dataset, evaluate, and iteratively add data to address specific weaknesses rather than trying to collect everything upfront.

**Q108. SFT vs preference tuning — difference?**
SFT (Supervised Fine-Tuning) trains the model to imitate specific target responses using a standard cross-entropy loss — it teaches the model what to say by showing it ideal (instruction, response) pairs. Preference tuning (DPO, RLHF, KTO) teaches the model which responses are better by showing it comparisons between preferred and rejected responses — it teaches relative quality rather than absolute targets. SFT is better for teaching specific formats, styles, and knowledge; preference tuning is better for alignment, safety, reducing verbosity, improving helpfulness, and making the model's output distribution match human preferences over qualities that are hard to demonstrate by example (like being concise, not hedging unnecessarily, or being honest about uncertainty). In the typical training pipeline, SFT comes first (teaching the model to follow instructions) and preference tuning comes second (refining the quality of those instruction-following responses).

**Q109. How do you handle multi-turn conversation data in fine-tuning?**
Multi-turn conversation data requires careful handling of loss masking, turn boundaries, and context management. The conversation is concatenated into a single sequence with special tokens marking turn boundaries (e.g., ChatML format: <|im_start|>user\n...<|im_end|><|im_start|>assistant\n...<|im_end|>). Critically, the loss should only be computed on assistant turns — user messages and system prompts are masked from the loss calculation (labels set to -100 in HuggingFace) so the model learns to generate responses, not to predict user messages. For long conversations that exceed the context window, you can either truncate from the beginning (losing early context), split into overlapping windows, or train only on the last few turns with earlier turns as context. You should also handle the conversation's causal structure: each assistant response should only see the context available at that point in the conversation. Frameworks like TRL, Axolotl, and Unsloth handle these mechanics automatically when given properly formatted conversation data.

**Q110. Learning rate scheduling strategies for fine-tuning?**
The most common schedule is cosine decay with warmup: the learning rate linearly increases from near-zero to the peak over 3–10% of total steps (warmup), then follows a cosine curve down to near-zero by the end of training. Peak learning rates for full fine-tuning are typically 1e-5 to 5e-5 (much lower than pre-training, to avoid catastrophic forgetting); for LoRA, 1e-4 to 3e-4 is standard (higher because fewer parameters need more aggressive updates). Constant learning rate with warmup is simpler and works surprisingly well for short fine-tuning runs. Linear decay is another option but cosine is generally preferred. For very short runs (<1 epoch), the warmup phase matters proportionally more — some practitioners skip warmup for very short runs or use a warmup ratio of just 1–3%. The key principle is that fine-tuning learning rates should be substantially lower than pre-training rates to preserve the model's existing knowledge, and the schedule should be matched to the training duration to avoid either under-training or oscillating around the optimum.

**Q111. Role of the reference model (frozen copy) in DPO/RLHF?**
The reference model is a frozen copy of the model at the start of alignment training (typically the SFT checkpoint), and it serves as an anchor to prevent the trained policy from deviating too far from reasonable behavior. In RLHF, a KL divergence penalty KL(π_θ || π_ref) is added to the reward, penalizing the policy for producing output distributions that diverge significantly from the reference — without this, the policy would exploit weaknesses in the reward model ("reward hacking") by producing degenerate outputs that game the reward. In DPO, the reference model appears explicitly in the loss: the log-probability ratios log(π_θ/π_ref) for chosen and rejected responses are compared, effectively measuring how much the trained model's preferences have shifted relative to the reference. The β hyperparameter controls how strongly the reference model constrains the training: higher β keeps the model closer to the reference (more conservative), lower β allows more deviation (more aggressive alignment). The reference model requires additional memory (one extra model copy), which is why methods like ORPO that eliminate it are attractive.

**Q112. How do you evaluate a fine-tuned model?**
Evaluate on multiple axes: (1) Task-specific metrics on a held-out test set (accuracy, F1, BLEU/ROUGE, exact match, whatever is appropriate for your task). (2) General capability benchmarks to check for catastrophic forgetting — run the model on standard benchmarks like MMLU, HellaSwag, or HumanEval and compare with the base model. (3) LLM-as-judge evaluation: use a strong model (GPT-4, Claude) to rate output quality on dimensions like helpfulness, accuracy, and coherence, comparing your fine-tuned model against the base model in head-to-head evaluations. (4) Human evaluation: have domain experts review a sample of outputs for quality, correctness, and safety — this remains the gold standard. (5) Safety/alignment testing: check that fine-tuning hasn't introduced harmful behaviors or degraded safety guardrails. (6) Edge case and adversarial testing: probe specific failure modes relevant to your use case. Track all metrics across fine-tuning checkpoints to select the best one, not just the final checkpoint. Tools like lm-evaluation-harness (EleutherAI) and AlpacaEval make benchmark evaluation straightforward.

**Q113. What is model merging (TIES, DARE, model soups)?**
Model merging combines the weights of multiple fine-tuned models (trained from the same base) into a single model without additional training, aiming to get the combined capabilities of all constituent models. Model Soups (Wortsman et al., 2022) simply averages the weights of models fine-tuned with different hyperparameters for improved robustness. TIES-Merging (Yadav et al., 2023) addresses interference between task-specific updates by: trimming small-magnitude deltas, resolving sign conflicts (when models disagree on which direction to update a parameter), and merging only the agreed-upon components. DARE (Yu et al., 2024) randomly drops a large fraction (e.g., 90%) of each model's fine-tuning deltas before merging and rescales the remaining ones, based on the observation that most individual parameter changes are redundant. These techniques enable combining a coding model, a math model, and a chat model into a single multitask model, and they are widely used in the open-source community (mergekit is the standard tool). The results can be surprisingly good, though they don't match training on the combined data.

**Q114. How to detect and prevent overfitting during fine-tuning?**
Detect overfitting by monitoring both training loss and validation loss (on a held-out set) throughout training — overfitting manifests as training loss continuing to decrease while validation loss starts increasing. Also track task-specific metrics on the validation set and general capability benchmarks at checkpoints. Prevention strategies: use a small learning rate and few epochs (1–3 epochs is typical; the more data you have, the more epochs you can use); apply LoRA/QLoRA rather than full fine-tuning (freezing most parameters acts as strong regularization); use dropout on LoRA layers (0.05–0.1); keep training data diverse and high-quality; implement early stopping based on validation metrics; use weight decay (0.01–0.1); and save checkpoints frequently to select the best one post-training. A practical red flag: if the model starts generating outputs that are suspiciously close verbatim copies of training examples, or if performance on held-out examples diverges sharply from training examples, you are likely overfitting.

**Q115. What is NEFTune (noise embeddings during fine-tuning)?**
NEFTune (Jain et al., 2023) adds uniform random noise to the input embeddings during fine-tuning: embedding = embedding + noise, where noise is sampled uniformly from [-α/√(L·d), α/√(L·d)], with L being sequence length, d the embedding dimension, and α a hyperparameter (typically 5–15). This surprisingly simple technique consistently improves instruction-following quality by 5–15% on benchmarks like AlpacaEval, with essentially zero implementation cost. The hypothesized mechanism is that noise injection acts as a regularizer that prevents the model from overfitting to the exact surface form of training examples, encouraging it to learn more robust representations and generalize better to novel instructions. NEFTune is supported in TRL (HuggingFace) and Unsloth with a single parameter flag, making it a low-risk, high-reward addition to any fine-tuning recipe.

**Q116. Axolotl vs Unsloth — when would you use each?**
Axolotl (OpenAccess AI Collective) is a comprehensive, highly configurable fine-tuning framework that supports virtually every training recipe (full fine-tuning, LoRA, QLoRA, DPO, RLHF), multiple data formats, multi-GPU/multi-node training via DeepSpeed and FSDP, and advanced features like sample packing and curriculum learning — use it when you need maximum flexibility, multi-GPU training, or complex training configurations. Unsloth is focused specifically on fast, memory-efficient single-GPU fine-tuning with LoRA/QLoRA, achieving 2–5x speedup through custom Triton kernels and optimized memory management — use it when you're training on a single GPU and want the fastest possible iteration cycle with minimal setup. In practice: Unsloth for rapid experimentation and resource-constrained settings (single consumer GPU), Axolotl for production fine-tuning runs, multi-GPU setups, and when you need features Unsloth doesn't support. Both integrate with HuggingFace and support the most common model architectures.

**Q117. Continued pre-training vs fine-tuning — when to use each?**
Continued pre-training (CPT) extends the model's next-token prediction training on a domain-specific corpus (raw text, not instruction-formatted), teaching the model new domain knowledge, terminology, and writing patterns before any instruction tuning. Use CPT when the target domain is significantly underrepresented in the base model's training data (e.g., specialized medical literature, proprietary codebases, non-English languages) and you have a large corpus (hundreds of millions to billions of tokens) of domain text. Fine-tuning (instruction tuning) teaches the model to follow instructions and produce formatted outputs from a much smaller dataset of (instruction, response) pairs. The typical pipeline for domain adaptation is: base model → continued pre-training on domain corpus → instruction fine-tuning on domain-specific instruction data → optional preference alignment. Skip CPT when the domain is well-represented in standard training data (e.g., general Python coding) or when you have insufficient domain text (<10M tokens). CPT requires a lower learning rate than initial pre-training to avoid catastrophic forgetting.

---

### Part 3: Mixture of Experts (MoE)

**Q118. What is a Mixture of Experts model? Basic architecture?**
A Mixture of Experts (MoE) model replaces the single dense feed-forward network (FFN) in each transformer layer with multiple parallel FFN "experts" and a gating/router network that selects which experts process each token. For each input token, the router computes a probability distribution over all experts and activates only the top-k (typically top-1 or top-2), so only a fraction of the model's total parameters are used per token. This decouples model capacity (total parameters, which determines knowledge storage) from computational cost (active parameters per token, which determines inference speed). For example, Mixtral 8x7B has 8 experts per layer with ~47B total parameters but activates only 2 experts per token, using ~13B active parameters — achieving performance comparable to much larger dense models at a fraction of the inference cost. The core architecture was introduced by Shazeer et al. (2017) and has been refined in Switch Transformer, GShard, Mixtral, and DeepSeek-MoE.

**Q119. How does the gating/router network work?**
The gating network is a simple learned linear layer that maps each token's hidden state to a probability distribution over N experts: gate(x) = softmax(W_g · x), where W_g ∈ R^(N × d) and x is the token's hidden representation. The top-k experts (by gating score) are selected, their corresponding gating weights are renormalized to sum to 1, and the final output is the weighted sum of the selected experts' outputs: output = Σ_i g_i · Expert_i(x), where g_i are the renormalized gate scores for the selected experts. Variations include: noisy top-k gating (adding noise before the softmax to encourage exploration during training), expert choice routing (where experts select their top-k tokens rather than tokens selecting experts, introduced by Zhou et al., 2022), and auxiliary load-balancing losses that penalize the router for sending too many tokens to the same expert. The router is trained end-to-end with the rest of the model via standard backpropagation.

**Q120. Total parameters vs active parameters — explain the distinction.**
Total parameters are all learnable weights in the model, including all experts across all MoE layers — this determines the model's knowledge capacity and storage requirements (disk and RAM). Active parameters are the subset used to process any single token, which includes the shared components (embeddings, attention layers, layer norms) plus only the k selected experts (out of N total) in each MoE layer. For Mixtral 8x7B: total parameters ≈ 47B (8 expert FFNs per MoE layer, each ~7B-model sized), active parameters ≈ 13B (shared attention + 2 out of 8 experts). This distinction matters because: inference speed and compute cost scale with active parameters (so Mixtral runs as fast as a 13B dense model), but memory must hold all total parameters (so Mixtral needs the memory of a 47B model unless experts are offloaded). This is MoE's fundamental value proposition: large-model quality at small-model inference cost, with the tradeoff of higher memory requirements.

**Q121. Load balancing problem in MoE — what is the auxiliary loss?**
The load balancing problem occurs when the router learns to send most tokens to a few "popular" experts while other experts are underutilized, wasting their capacity and degrading overall model quality — in the extreme case ("winner-take-all"), some experts receive no tokens and their parameters become dead weights. The auxiliary loss (introduced by Shazeer et al., 2017 and refined in Switch Transformer) penalizes this imbalance by adding a term to the training loss proportional to the product of each expert's average gating probability and the fraction of tokens routed to it: L_aux = α · N · Σ_i (f_i · P_i), where f_i is the fraction of tokens dispatched to expert i, P_i is the average router probability for expert i, and α is a weighting coefficient (typically 0.01–0.1). This encourages uniform distribution of tokens across experts. The coefficient α must be tuned carefully: too low allows imbalance, too high forces artificial balance that hurts quality by routing tokens to suboptimal experts.

**Q122. How does Mixtral 8x7B work? How many experts per token?**
Mixtral 8x7B (Mistral AI, 2023) has 32 transformer layers where each layer's FFN is replaced by 8 expert FFN modules, with a router selecting the top-2 experts per token. The attention layers are shared across all tokens (not expert-specific). Each expert is a standard SwiGLU FFN with the same architecture as Mistral 7B's FFN, giving total parameters of ~47B but only ~13B active parameters per forward pass (shared attention layers + 2 experts' FFNs). The router's top-2 gating weights are renormalized to sum to 1, and the outputs are the weighted sum of the two selected experts. Mixtral uses a sliding window attention of 4096 tokens with a 32K total context window. It achieves performance competitive with LLaMA-2 70B (a 5x larger dense model in active parameters) on most benchmarks, validating the MoE efficiency thesis. It also uses grouped-query attention (GQA) to reduce KV cache memory. The architecture has become the template for many subsequent open-source MoE models.

**Q123. Expert parallelism in distributed MoE training/serving?**
Expert parallelism distributes different experts across different devices, so each device holds a subset of experts. When processing a batch of tokens, the router determines which experts each token needs, and an all-to-all communication operation shuffles tokens to the devices holding their assigned experts. After expert computation, another all-to-all sends the results back. This is combined with other parallelism strategies: data parallelism (different devices process different batches), tensor parallelism (a single expert split across devices), and pipeline parallelism (different layers on different devices). The key challenge is the all-to-all communication overhead, which can become a bottleneck — GShard (Lepikhin et al., 2021) and Switch Transformer (Fedus et al., 2021) introduced techniques to minimize this, including capacity factors (limiting the max tokens per expert to bound communication) and auxiliary losses for balanced routing. DeepSpeed-MoE and Megaton-LM provide practical implementations of expert parallelism for training and inference.

**Q124. Why are MoE models more efficient at inference than dense models of same total params?**
Because only k out of N experts are activated per token, the actual computation (FLOPs) is proportional to active parameters, not total parameters. For Mixtral 8x7B with 47B total params activating 2/8 experts: the FFN computation per token is 2/8 = 25% of what it would be if all experts were used, while the attention layers (shared, not expert-based) are the same regardless. This means Mixtral processes each token with roughly the same FLOPs as a 13B dense model while having the knowledge capacity of a much larger model. The intuition is that different tokens need different types of processing — a code token might benefit from "code-specialized" experts while a natural language token uses "language-specialized" experts — so most of the 47B parameters are irrelevant for any given token and don't need to be computed. The tradeoff is that all 47B parameters must reside in memory (or be efficiently swapped), making MoE models more memory-intensive than dense models of the same active parameter count.

**Q125. Challenges of fine-tuning MoE models?**
Fine-tuning MoE models presents several unique challenges: (1) Memory — all experts must be loaded even though only a subset is active, requiring substantially more GPU memory than a dense model of equivalent active size. (2) Expert imbalance — fine-tuning on narrow data can collapse routing, causing most tokens to go to the same few experts and effectively destroying the MoE structure. (3) Load balancing — the auxiliary loss used during pre-training must be carefully maintained during fine-tuning; without it, experts can become highly imbalanced. (4) LoRA application — you can apply LoRA to shared attention layers (straightforward) and/or to expert FFNs (increases total adapter parameters by N experts, but each adapter is small). (5) Catastrophic forgetting can be more severe if expert specialization is disrupted. (6) Small datasets particularly risk expert collapse because there isn't enough diversity to maintain balanced routing. Practical mitigation includes using LoRA on all layers (attention + experts), maintaining the load balancing loss, using moderate learning rates, and monitoring expert utilization during training.

**Q126. DeepSeek's MoE innovations — what did they introduce?**
DeepSeek introduced several important MoE innovations across their DeepSeek-MoE and DeepSeek-V2/V3 models. Key contributions include: (1) Fine-grained experts — using many more, smaller experts (e.g., 64 or 160 instead of 8) with more active experts per token (e.g., 6 out of 64), which provides more flexible and precise specialization. (2) Shared experts — designating some experts as always-active for all tokens (capturing common knowledge) alongside routed experts (capturing specialized knowledge), which improves baseline quality and reduces the need for every routed expert to redundantly store common information. (3) Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) — compressing KV cache into a low-rank latent space, dramatically reducing KV cache memory for long-context inference. (4) Auxiliary-loss-free load balancing — using a bias term in the router instead of an auxiliary loss to balance expert utilization, which provides better balancing without the quality-balance tradeoff of auxiliary losses. These innovations enabled DeepSeek-V3 (671B total / 37B active) to achieve frontier-model performance at dramatically lower training cost.

**Q127. "Expert collapse" problem — what is it and how is it addressed?**
Expert collapse occurs when the router converges to always selecting the same small subset of experts, effectively underutilizing or completely ignoring other experts, which degrades the model to a smaller dense model and wastes the capacity of unused experts. In the extreme case, a model with 8 experts might only ever use 2–3 of them. This happens because routing is a self-reinforcing process: experts that receive more tokens get more gradient updates, become better, and attract even more tokens, while underutilized experts stagnate and become increasingly irrelevant. Solutions include: auxiliary load-balancing losses (the most common approach), adding noise to gating scores during training to encourage exploration, expert choice routing (experts pick their tokens, guaranteeing equal load by construction), dropout on routing scores, periodic expert reinitialization (copying the weights of a strong expert into a collapsed one), and DeepSeek's bias-based balancing approach. Monitoring expert utilization during training (tracking what fraction of tokens each expert processes) is essential for early detection.

**Q128. MoE + quantization — special considerations?**
Quantizing MoE models has unique considerations compared to dense models. Since all experts must reside in memory but most are inactive per token, MoE models benefit disproportionately from quantization — reducing Mixtral 8x7B from fp16 (~94GB) to 4-bit (~24GB) makes it runnable on consumer GPUs. However, not all experts may be equally amenable to quantization: less-used experts may have different weight distributions and quantize differently than popular ones. Per-expert calibration data is important — if calibration data doesn't activate all experts equally, some experts will be poorly quantized. The router weights and shared attention layers should generally be kept at higher precision since they affect every token, while expert FFNs can be aggressively quantized. Frameworks like GPTQ, AWQ, and llama.cpp handle MoE quantization well. Additionally, because each token only uses 2 experts, the effective per-token quality impact of quantization may be higher in MoE than in dense models (errors in the 2 active experts cannot be compensated by other experts).

**Q129. MoE vs dense models for same compute budget — what does research show?**
Research consistently shows that MoE models outperform dense models at the same compute (FLOP) budget by a significant margin, both for training and inference. The Switch Transformer paper (Fedus et al., 2021) demonstrated that MoE models achieve the same quality as dense models using 2–4x fewer training FLOPs, or equivalently achieve substantially better quality at the same FLOP budget. Mixtral 8x7B (~13B active params) matches or exceeds LLaMA-2 70B (dense) on most benchmarks while using ~5x fewer FLOPs per token. DeepSeek-V3 (37B active out of 671B total) competes with models that required orders of magnitude more training compute. The efficiency advantage comes from the observation that not all parameters need to be engaged for every input — conditional computation allows the model to specialize. The tradeoff is higher total parameter count (more memory) and communication overhead in distributed training. For a fixed memory budget, dense models may still be preferred; for a fixed compute budget, MoE is generally superior.

**Q130. Switch Transformer and its relation to modern MoE?**
The Switch Transformer (Fedus et al., 2021, Google) simplified MoE by routing each token to only a single expert (top-1) instead of top-2, arguing that the simplicity and reduced communication overhead outweighed the loss from using fewer experts per token. Key contributions that influenced modern MoE include: (1) demonstrating that top-1 routing works well, simplifying the architecture. (2) Introducing capacity factors that limit the maximum number of tokens an expert can process, handling the variable load problem and reducing communication in distributed settings. (3) Showing MoE scaling to trillions of parameters (Switch-C had 1.6T parameters). (4) Systematically studying expert count, demonstrating that more experts consistently help even with top-1 routing. (5) Providing practical recipes for stable MoE training (selective precision, higher auxiliary loss coefficients). Modern MoE models like Mixtral returned to top-2 routing for quality, but Switch Transformer's training stability techniques, capacity factor concept, and scaling analyses remain foundational to the field.

**Q131. Routing strategies — top-1, top-2, expert choice routing?**
Top-1 routing (Switch Transformer) sends each token to its single highest-scored expert — simplest, cheapest computation, but each token gets only one perspective and load can be highly imbalanced. Top-2 routing (Mixtral, GShard) sends each token to its two highest-scored experts and combines their outputs via weighted sum — better quality since each token benefits from two specialized views, but doubles expert computation. Top-k generalizes to any number of active experts, with diminishing returns beyond 2–4 for most architectures. Expert choice routing (Zhou et al., 2022) inverts the paradigm: instead of tokens choosing experts, each expert selects its top-k tokens from the batch, guaranteeing perfectly balanced load by construction. This eliminates the load balancing problem entirely but introduces the complication that some tokens may be selected by zero or many experts, requiring either dropping (underserved tokens use a shared FFN) or variable computation. Expert choice routing also requires batched processing and doesn't naturally work for single-token autoregressive inference. In practice, top-2 routing with auxiliary load balancing remains the most common approach in deployed models.

**Q132. What is RAG? Why preferred over fine-tuning for knowledge tasks?**
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) combines a retrieval system with an LLM: given a query, relevant documents are fetched from an external knowledge base and injected into the prompt context, so the model generates answers grounded in actual source material. RAG is preferred over fine-tuning for knowledge-intensive tasks because it doesn't require expensive retraining when information changes — you simply update the document store — and it provides source attribution, reduces hallucination by grounding outputs in retrieved evidence, and avoids the risk of catastrophic forgetting that comes with fine-tuning. Fine-tuning is better for teaching the model new behaviors or styles, while RAG excels at factual recall over large, evolving corpora. The foundational paper is Lewis et al. (2020), "Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks."

**Q133. Full RAG pipeline — document ingestion to answer generation.**
A complete RAG pipeline has three phases: ingestion, retrieval, and generation. During ingestion, raw documents (PDFs, HTML, databases) are parsed, cleaned, split into chunks, converted to vector embeddings via an embedding model (e.g., OpenAI `text-embedding-3-large`, Cohere Embed), and stored in a vector database alongside metadata. At query time, the user's question is embedded with the same model, a similarity search retrieves the top-k most relevant chunks, and optionally a reranker (cross-encoder) re-scores them for precision. Finally, the retrieved chunks are inserted into the LLM's prompt as context, and the model generates an answer grounded in that evidence. Key design decisions include chunking strategy, embedding model choice, number of retrieved documents, whether to use hybrid search (dense + sparse), and how to format the context in the prompt.

**Q134. Embedding models for RAG — compare common options.**
OpenAI's `text-embedding-3-large` (3072 dims) offers strong general-purpose performance with native dimension reduction via Matryoshka representation; `text-embedding-3-small` is cheaper and faster with modest quality tradeoff. Cohere's Embed v3 supports multiple input types (search_document vs search_query) and natively handles multilingual content well. Open-source options include BGE (BAAI), E5-Mistral-7B, and GTE (Alibaba), which top the MTEB leaderboard and can be self-hosted for data privacy and cost control. Sentence-transformers (`all-MiniLM-L6-v2`) remains popular for lightweight use cases but lags behind newer models on retrieval benchmarks. For domain-specific tasks, fine-tuning an embedding model on your own query-document pairs (using hard negatives) typically yields the biggest quality gains. Check the MTEB leaderboard on Hugging Face for current rankings.

**Q135. Document chunking strategies — approaches and tradeoffs?**
The main strategies are: fixed-size chunking (e.g., 512 tokens with overlap), which is simple but can split sentences mid-thought; recursive character splitting (LangChain's default), which tries paragraph then sentence then character boundaries; semantic chunking, which uses embedding similarity between consecutive sentences to detect natural topic boundaries; and document-structure-aware chunking, which respects headings, sections, and tables. Overlap (typically 10-20% of chunk size) helps preserve context at boundaries. Smaller chunks improve retrieval precision but lose surrounding context; larger chunks preserve context but dilute relevance signals and consume more of the LLM's context window. The best approach depends on document type: structured documents (legal, technical) benefit from section-aware chunking, while conversational or narrative text works well with semantic chunking. LangChain and LlamaIndex both provide configurable text splitters worth exploring.

**Q136. Optimal chunk size — how to determine?**
There is no universal optimal chunk size — it depends on the embedding model's training context length, the nature of your documents, and the types of queries you expect. Empirically, 256-512 tokens works well for factoid questions requiring precise retrieval, while 512-1024 tokens suits questions needing broader context. The best approach is to run an evaluation: create a test set of queries with known relevant passages, chunk your documents at several sizes (e.g., 128, 256, 512, 1024), measure retrieval recall and downstream answer quality, and pick the best tradeoff. Also consider that your embedding model has a sweet spot — models trained on short passages (like `all-MiniLM`) degrade on long chunks, while models like E5-Mistral handle up to 4096 tokens. Using a parent-child chunking strategy can sidestep the tradeoff entirely: retrieve on small chunks but pass the larger parent chunk to the LLM.

**Q137. Vector similarity — cosine similarity, dot product, Euclidean distance.**
Cosine similarity measures the angle between two vectors (range -1 to 1), making it invariant to magnitude and the most common choice for normalized embeddings — if vectors are unit-normalized, cosine similarity equals dot product. Dot product (inner product) captures both direction and magnitude, which matters when embedding models encode relevance partly through vector norm (e.g., some models produce longer vectors for more "important" content). Euclidean (L2) distance measures straight-line distance in embedding space; it's equivalent to cosine for normalized vectors but sensitive to magnitude otherwise. In practice, most embedding models produce normalized vectors, so cosine and dot product give identical rankings. Use cosine similarity as the default; switch to dot product if your model explicitly encodes information in vector magnitude. Pinecone, Qdrant, and other vector DBs let you select the metric at index creation time.

**Q138. Compare vector databases: Pinecone, Qdrant, Weaviate, ChromaDB, Milvus, pgvector.**
Pinecone is a fully managed cloud service — zero ops, scales effortlessly, supports hybrid search with sparse-dense vectors, but is proprietary and can get expensive at scale. Qdrant (Rust-based) offers excellent performance, rich filtering, payload storage, and can be self-hosted or cloud-managed; it's my go-to for production. Weaviate provides a GraphQL API, built-in vectorization modules, and multi-tenancy but has a steeper learning curve. ChromaDB is the simplest to start with (pip install, in-memory or persistent), ideal for prototyping and small-scale apps, but not designed for large-scale production. Milvus (C++/Go) handles billion-scale vector workloads with GPU acceleration and is battle-tested at large companies, though operationally heavier. pgvector extends PostgreSQL with vector similarity search — great when you want to keep everything in one database and your scale is moderate (millions of vectors), but lacks the advanced indexing and filtering of dedicated vector DBs.

**Q139. What is hybrid search? Dense + sparse (BM25) retrieval?**
Hybrid search combines dense vector retrieval (semantic similarity via embeddings) with sparse retrieval (lexical matching, typically BM25) to get the best of both worlds. Dense retrieval excels at semantic understanding — finding passages that mean the same thing as the query even with different words — but can miss exact keyword matches, acronyms, or rare terms. BM25 is great at precise term matching but has no semantic understanding. In hybrid search, both retrievers return scored results which are then combined using a fusion method like Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) or weighted linear combination. This consistently outperforms either method alone across benchmarks. Most modern vector databases support it natively: Pinecone with sparse-dense vectors, Qdrant with FastEmbed + sparse vectors, Weaviate with BM25 + vector modules. For implementation, you can also run BM25 (via Elasticsearch or a library like rank_bm25) alongside your vector DB and fuse results yourself.

**Q140. Re-ranking in RAG — cross-encoder rerankers?**
Re-ranking is a second-stage retrieval step where a more powerful model re-scores the initial top-k retrieved candidates to improve precision. The typical approach uses a cross-encoder — a model that takes (query, document) as a single input pair and outputs a relevance score, unlike bi-encoders which encode query and document independently. Cross-encoders are far more accurate because they can attend across query and document tokens jointly, capturing fine-grained interactions, but they're too slow to run over the entire corpus — hence the two-stage pattern: fast bi-encoder retrieval over millions of documents, then cross-encoder reranking of the top 20-100 results. Popular rerankers include Cohere Rerank (API-based, easy to use), BGE-Reranker, and `cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM` from sentence-transformers. Reranking typically improves RAG answer quality by 5-15% on relevance metrics and is one of the highest-ROI improvements you can add to a RAG pipeline.

**Q141. "Lost in the middle" in RAG — how to address?**
The "lost in the middle" problem, documented by Liu et al. (2023), shows that LLMs pay most attention to information at the beginning and end of their context window, often ignoring or underweighting content placed in the middle. For RAG this means that if you stuff many retrieved chunks into the prompt, the model may miss the most relevant one if it happens to land in the middle positions. Mitigation strategies include: limiting the number of retrieved chunks (quality over quantity), placing the most relevant chunks at the beginning or end of the context, re-ranking to ensure only highly relevant passages are included, using a map-reduce approach where each chunk is processed independently then answers are merged, and structuring prompts so the model is explicitly instructed to consider all provided context. Some newer models (GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3+) have improved on this issue with better long-context handling, but it remains a consideration for any RAG system.

**Q142. Multi-hop questions requiring info from multiple documents?**
Multi-hop questions (e.g., "What is the GDP of the country where the inventor of the telephone was born?") require reasoning across multiple retrieved passages. Standard single-retrieval RAG fails because no single chunk contains the full answer. Solutions include: query decomposition, where the complex question is broken into sub-questions ("Who invented the telephone?" then "Where was Alexander Graham Bell born?" then "What is Scotland's GDP?") each retrieved independently; iterative retrieval, where the LLM generates follow-up queries based on partial answers; and agentic RAG, where an agent plans and executes a multi-step retrieval strategy using tools. LangGraph and LlamaIndex both support building these multi-step retrieval chains. Knowledge graphs can also help by encoding entity relationships that bridge documents. The key insight is that the retrieval strategy itself needs to be multi-step, not just the reasoning.

**Q143. What is HyDE (Hypothetical Document Embeddings)?**
HyDE, proposed by Gao et al. (2022), addresses the query-document mismatch problem: user queries are often short and stylistically different from the documents they seek. Instead of embedding the raw query, HyDE first asks an LLM to generate a hypothetical answer document (which may contain inaccuracies but captures the right semantic space), then embeds that hypothetical document and uses it for vector similarity search. Because the generated text resembles actual documents in vocabulary and structure, it often matches better than the original query embedding. HyDE works particularly well for abstract or underspecified queries but adds latency (one LLM call before retrieval) and can mislead retrieval if the LLM's hypothetical answer goes in the wrong direction. It's most useful when queries are terse or domain-specific. The technique can be combined with multi-query retrieval for robustness — generate multiple hypothetical documents, retrieve for each, and merge results.

**Q144. Parent-child / hierarchical chunking strategies?**
Parent-child (or hierarchical) chunking creates two levels: small "child" chunks optimized for precise retrieval (e.g., 128-256 tokens) and larger "parent" chunks that provide the broader context the LLM needs to generate good answers (e.g., 1024-2048 tokens or full sections). During retrieval, the system searches against child chunk embeddings for high-precision matching, then returns the corresponding parent chunk to the LLM. This elegantly resolves the chunk-size tradeoff: small chunks give better retrieval recall while large chunks give the LLM sufficient context. LlamaIndex implements this natively with its `AutoMergingRetriever` — when multiple child chunks from the same parent are retrieved, they merge back into the parent. You can also extend this to multiple levels (sentence → paragraph → section → document). The main implementation consideration is maintaining reliable parent-child mappings in your vector store via metadata.

**Q145. Query decomposition / multi-query retrieval?**
Query decomposition breaks a complex query into simpler sub-queries that are each easier to retrieve for, while multi-query retrieval generates multiple reformulations of the same question to overcome vocabulary mismatch and improve recall. For decomposition, an LLM splits "Compare the economic impacts of COVID-19 on the US and EU" into separate queries about each region's economic impact, retrieves for each, then synthesizes. For multi-query, the LLM generates paraphrases (e.g., the original query plus 3-4 rephrasings or related angles), runs retrieval for all, and merges results using union or RRF. LangChain's `MultiQueryRetriever` and LlamaIndex's `SubQuestionQueryEngine` implement these patterns out of the box. Both techniques improve retrieval recall significantly, especially for complex or ambiguous queries, at the cost of additional LLM calls and retrieval latency. They compose well with reranking: retrieve broadly via multiple queries, then rerank the merged set to select the best passages.

**Q146. How to evaluate RAG? (faithfulness, relevance, recall)**
RAG evaluation requires measuring both retrieval quality and generation quality independently. Retrieval metrics include context recall (did we retrieve all relevant chunks?), context precision (are retrieved chunks actually relevant?), and MRR/NDCG from information retrieval. Generation metrics include faithfulness/groundedness (does the answer only use information from the retrieved context, not hallucinate?), answer relevance (does the answer actually address the question?), and answer correctness (is the answer factually right?). You need a test set of questions with known answers and ideally known relevant passages. LLM-as-judge is now the standard approach for faithfulness and relevance — a strong LLM checks whether each claim in the answer is supported by the context. Tools like RAGAS, DeepEval, and LangSmith automate these evaluations. The key insight is to evaluate retrieval and generation separately so you know where to focus improvements.

**Q147. What is RAGAS framework?**
RAGAS (Retrieval Augmented Generation Assessment) is an open-source evaluation framework specifically designed for RAG pipelines, introduced by Shahul et al. (2023). It provides automated metrics that use LLMs as judges: faithfulness (what fraction of claims in the answer are supported by the context), answer relevancy (how relevant is the answer to the question), context precision (are the relevant chunks ranked higher?), and context recall (what fraction of the ground-truth answer is attributable to retrieved context). RAGAS can work in a reference-free mode (no ground-truth answers needed, just questions) for faithfulness and relevancy, which makes it practical for continuous monitoring. You provide your RAG pipeline's outputs — question, retrieved contexts, generated answer, and optionally ground truth — and RAGAS scores each dimension 0-1. It integrates with LangChain, LlamaIndex, and CI/CD pipelines. Install via `pip install ragas` and read the docs at docs.ragas.io for detailed usage.

**Q148. Graph RAG — knowledge graph integration?**
Graph RAG augments traditional vector-based retrieval with knowledge graphs (KGs) that capture explicit entity-entity relationships, enabling structured reasoning that pure embedding similarity cannot achieve. In the Microsoft Research Graph RAG approach (2024), documents are first processed to extract entities and relationships into a graph, communities of related entities are detected and summarized, and at query time the system can traverse these graph structures to answer global questions that require synthesizing information across the entire corpus — something standard RAG struggles with. More generally, graph RAG can mean using a KG (like Neo4j) alongside a vector store: the vector store handles semantic similarity while graph traversal handles multi-hop relational queries ("which companies in our portfolio are connected to this person?"). Tools like LlamaIndex's `KnowledgeGraphIndex` and Neo4j's vector+graph integrations support this pattern. Graph RAG is especially valuable for domains with rich relational structure: legal, biomedical, financial, and organizational data.

**Q149. Agentic RAG — agents deciding when/how to retrieve?**
Agentic RAG gives an LLM agent the autonomy to decide when, what, and how to retrieve rather than always following a fixed retrieve-then-generate pipeline. The agent can choose among multiple retrieval tools (different indexes, databases, APIs, web search), decide whether retrieval is even necessary for a given query, formulate and reformulate queries based on intermediate results, and perform multi-step retrieval with reasoning between steps. For example, an agent might first query a vector store, determine the results are insufficient, decompose the question, search a different index, then synthesize. This is implemented with frameworks like LangGraph (where retrieval is a tool the agent invokes) or LlamaIndex's agent framework. Agentic RAG dramatically outperforms static RAG on complex queries but adds latency, cost, and nondeterminism. The key design challenge is balancing agent autonomy with reliability — guardrails, timeouts, and fallback strategies are essential for production use.

**Q150. Contextual retrieval / contextual embeddings (Anthropic's approach)?**
Anthropic's Contextual Retrieval (2024) addresses the fundamental problem that individual chunks lose their surrounding context when embedded independently, degrading retrieval quality. The technique prepends a short, LLM-generated contextual description to each chunk before embedding — for example, a chunk from a financial report's revenue section would get a prefix like "This chunk is from Acme Corp's Q3 2024 earnings report, specifically the revenue breakdown section." This gives the embedding model awareness of where the chunk fits in the broader document. Anthropic showed this reduces retrieval failure rates by 49% when combined with BM25 hybrid search and reranking. The approach uses prompt caching to make the LLM calls affordable (since the full document is the same across all its chunks, only the chunk-specific instruction varies). It's conceptually simple and highly effective — one of the highest-impact, lowest-complexity improvements to any chunking strategy.

**Q151. Multi-modal RAG (text + images + tables)?**
Multi-modal RAG extends retrieval to handle not just text but images, tables, charts, and other non-text content that traditional RAG systems typically ignore or poorly convert. There are two main approaches: (1) use a multi-modal embedding model (like CLIP, Jina CLIP, or Voyage multimodal) that projects both text and images into the same vector space, enabling cross-modal retrieval where a text query can retrieve relevant images; and (2) convert everything to text — use vision-language models (GPT-4o, Claude) to generate detailed text descriptions of images and structured extractions of tables, then embed those descriptions alongside regular text chunks. For tables specifically, preserving structure matters: embed both a natural language summary and the raw structured data. At generation time, you pass retrieved images directly to a multimodal LLM along with text chunks. ColPali (see Q152) is an emerging approach that embeds document page images directly without any text extraction step, which is particularly effective for visually rich documents.

**Q152. ColBERT/ColPali — late interaction retrieval?**
ColBERT (Contextualized Late Interaction over BERT) introduced the late interaction paradigm: instead of compressing the entire query and document into single vectors (bi-encoder) or processing them jointly (cross-encoder), it encodes them independently into per-token embeddings, then computes relevance via a lightweight MaxSim operation — each query token's embedding is matched against all document token embeddings, taking the maximum similarity, then summing across query tokens. This preserves fine-grained token-level matching (like cross-encoders) while maintaining the scalability of independent encoding (document embeddings can be precomputed and indexed). ColBERT v2 improves storage efficiency with residual compression. ColPali extends this concept to vision: it uses a vision-language model (PaliGemma) to embed document page images directly as patch-level token embeddings, then applies the same late interaction matching. This eliminates the need for OCR, layout detection, or chunking entirely — you just embed page screenshots and retrieve against them, which is revolutionary for visually complex documents like slides, forms, and scientific papers.

**Q153. How to prevent hallucination in RAG? Grounded generation?**
Preventing hallucination in RAG requires a multi-layered approach across retrieval, generation, and verification. At the retrieval level: ensure high recall so the model has the right information, use reranking to push the most relevant content to the top, and set minimum similarity thresholds to avoid injecting irrelevant context that can confuse the model. At the prompt level: explicitly instruct the model to only use provided context, to say "I don't know" when the context is insufficient, and to cite specific passages. At the generation level: use lower temperature settings, request structured outputs with explicit source references, and employ chain-of-thought prompting where the model first identifies relevant context then generates the answer. Post-generation: implement a verification step where a second LLM call (or the same model) checks whether each claim in the answer is supported by the retrieved context — this is essentially runtime faithfulness evaluation. Tools like Guardrails AI and NeMo Guardrails can automate these checks. The most impactful single intervention is typically improving retrieval quality — if the right information is in the context, hallucination rates drop dramatically.

**Q154. Reciprocal rank fusion (RRF) for combining results?**
Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) is a simple, effective method for merging ranked result lists from multiple retrieval systems (e.g., dense vector search and BM25 in hybrid search, or multiple query reformulations). For each document appearing in any result list, its RRF score is computed as the sum of 1/(k + rank_i) across all lists where it appears, where k is a constant (typically 60) that dampens the influence of high-ranking positions. The key advantage of RRF is that it's score-agnostic — it only uses rank positions, so it seamlessly combines retrievers with incomparable score distributions (e.g., cosine similarity values and BM25 scores). It consistently outperforms simple score normalization and linear interpolation in practice. RRF is used natively in Elasticsearch and is straightforward to implement manually in about 10 lines of Python. The k parameter controls how much weight top-ranked results get versus lower-ranked ones — higher k makes the distribution flatter. The original paper is by Cormack, Clarke, and Buettcher (2009).

**Q155. Compare the Llama family (1 through 4). Key changes between versions?**
Llama 1 (Feb 2023) demonstrated that smaller, well-trained open models could match much larger ones, training 7B-65B parameter models on 1-1.4T tokens of public data. Llama 2 (Jul 2023) scaled to 70B, doubled context to 4096 tokens, introduced RLHF-tuned chat variants, and switched to Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) at 70B for faster inference. Llama 3 (Apr 2024) was a major leap: new 128K-token vocabulary tokenizer, GQA across all sizes, trained on 15T+ tokens (massively "over-training" relative to Chinchilla laws), 8K context (expandable to 128K for 3.1), and an 8B model that matched Llama 2 70B. Llama 3.1 405B became the largest open-weight model at the time and matched GPT-4 class performance. Llama 4 (2025) introduced a mixture-of-experts architecture for the first time in the family with Scout (17B active / 109B total, 16 experts) and Maverick (17B active / 400B total, 128 experts), achieving much better efficiency per active parameter. Each generation has pushed the boundaries of what open-weight models can achieve relative to closed APIs.

**Q156. Mistral architecture innovations vs Llama?**
Mistral introduced several architectural innovations that differentiated it from Llama. Mistral 7B (Sep 2023) used Sliding Window Attention (SWA) with a 4096-token window that enables each token to attend to a limited local context while still propagating information across layers for an effective receptive field much larger than the window — this reduces memory and compute versus full attention. It also used GQA from the start at 7B scale.
